---
<!-- def-1: body_md -->
\textbf{The Nahm--Kirchhoff} moduli space of a quiver $\Gamma$ is the quotient \[
 \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \coloneqq \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)/\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma).
 \]

 Finally, note that the action of $\mathcal{G}(\Gamma)$ descends to an action of the quotient group \[
 \mathcal{G}(\Gamma)/\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma) \cong G^{\partial \Gamma}
 \]
 on $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma).$
<!-- end def-1 -->
<!-- thm-1: body_md -->
\label{smooth quotient}
The subset $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ is a Banach submanifold of $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma),$ and the quotient \[
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \coloneqq \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)/\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma) 
\]
admits a unique smooth structure such that the projection $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \longrightarrow \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ is a smooth submersion. Moreover, $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ is finite-dimensional with $\dim(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)) = 4(|E| - |\Gamma_{\text{int}}|)\dim(G).$
<!-- end thm-1 -->
<!-- lem-1: body_md -->
\label{useful lemma}
    Let $W$ be a finite-dimensional real vector space and $B \in C^{0}(I,\text{End}(W)).$ Define \begin{align*}
    R: C^{1}(I,W)& \longrightarrow C^{0}(I,W) \times W^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}\\ x& \longmapsto 
    \Bigl( \dot{x} + Bx, \Bigl( \sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)} x_{e}(a_e) - \sum_{e \in t^{-1}(v)} x_{e}(b_e) \Bigr)_{v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}} \Bigr).  
    \end{align*}
    Then $R$ is surjective and $\dim \text{Ker}(R) = (|E| - |\Gamma_{\text{int}}|)\dim(W).$
<!-- end lem-1 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{regular value}
    The subset $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \subset \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ is a Banach submanifold.
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{useful lemma}, the differential 
\begin{align*}
    d\psi_{A}: \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)& \longrightarrow C^{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}^{3}) \times (\mathfrak{g}^{3})^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}\\
    X& \longmapsto \Bigl( \dot{X}_{\bar{i}} + [A_0,X_{\bar{i}}] + [X_0,A_{\bar{i}}] + [X_{\overline{i+1}},A_{\overline{i+2}}] + [A_{\overline{i+1}},X_{\overline{i+2}}], \Bigl( \sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)} X^{e}_{\bar{i}}(a_e) - \sum_{e\in t^{-1}(v)} X^{e}_{\bar{i}} (b_e) \Bigr) \Bigr), 
\end{align*}
is surjective for all $A \in \psi^{-1}(0).$ Hence $0$ is a regular value of $\psi$, and $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ is a Banach submanifold of $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma).$
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{proper gauge}
    $\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma)$ acts freely and properly on $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma).$
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
This follows from \cite[Proposition 3.4]{maiza2026lax} and \cite[Lemma 3.5]{maiza2026lax}.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
We have $$\langle D_{A}u,Y \rangle = \langle u,L(Y) \rangle,$$ for all $u\in C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g})$ and $Y \in T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma).$
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Using integration by parts and the $\operatorname{Ad}$-invariance of the scalar product, one gets \begin{align*}
        \langle D_{A}u, Y \rangle &= \int_{I} \langle [u,A_0] - \dot{u},Y_{0} \rangle + \langle [u,A_1],Y_1 \rangle + \langle [u,A_2],Y_2 \rangle + \langle [u,A_3],Y_3 \rangle dt\\ =& \int_{I} \langle u,\dot{Y}_{0} \rangle + \sum_{i=0}^{3} \langle [u,A_i],Y_i \rangle dt + \sum_{e\in E} (\langle u_{e}(a_e),Y^{e}_{0}(a_e) \rangle - \langle u_{e}(b_e),Y^{e}_{0}(b_e)\rangle)\\ =& \int_{I} \langle u(t),\sum_{i=0}^{3} [A_{i},Y_i] \rangle dt + \sum_{v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}} \langle u(v), \sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)} Y^{e}_{0}(a_e) - \sum_{e\in t^{-1}(v)} Y^{e}_{0}(b_e) \rangle\\ =& \langle u,L(Y) \rangle = \langle u,D^{*}_{A}Y \rangle.     
    \end{align*}
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- prop-4: body_md -->
\label{prop 3.6}
The operator \begin{align*}
L_{A}\coloneqq D^{*}_{A}D_{A}: C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g})& \longrightarrow C^{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}) \times \mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}\\
u& \longrightarrow \bigl( -\nabla^{2}_{A_0}u - \sum_{i=1}^{3} \operatorname{ad}^{2}_{A_i}u, (\delta_{v}(\dot{u}))_{v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}} \bigr),
\end{align*}
is an isomorphism of Banach spaces, where $\delta_{v}(\dot{u}) = \sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)} \dot{u}^{e}(a_e) - \sum_{e\in t^{-1}(v)}\dot{u}^{e}(b_e).$ In particular, \[
T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) = \text{im} D_A \oplus \ker D^{*}_A,
\]
is a closed, topological direct sum.
<!-- end prop-4 -->
<!-- lem-2: body_md -->
\label{oper}
The operator $L = D^{*}_{A}$ is surjective, and \[
\dim \text{Ker} D^{*}_{A} = 4(|E| - |\Gamma_{\text{int}}|)\dim(\mathfrak{g}).
\]
<!-- end lem-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Define the operator 
\begin{align*}
R: \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)& \longrightarrow C^{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}^{4})\times (\mathfrak{g}^{4})^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}\\
Y& \longmapsto \bigl(d_{A}\psi^{1}(Y), d_{A}\psi^{2}(Y),d_{A}\psi^{3}(Y),(D^{*}_{A}(Y))_{1}, (\delta_{v}(Y_1),\delta_{v}(Y_2),\delta_{v}(Y_3),\delta_{v}(Y_0))_{v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}} \bigr).
\end{align*}
By Lemma \ref{useful lemma}, $R$ is surjective. Moreover, \[
\text{Ker} R = \{ Y\in \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}, Y \in T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \text{ and } D^{*}_{A}Y = 0\} = \text{Ker} D^{*}_{A}.
\]

Then, $D^{*}_{A}$ is surjective and $\dim \text{Ker} D^{*}_{A} = \dim \text{Ker} R = 4(|E| - |\Gamma_{\text{int}}|)\dim(\mathfrak{g}).$
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lem-3: body_md -->
\label{injectivity}
    The operator $L_A = D^{*}_{A}D_{A}$ is injective.
<!-- end lem-3 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
This follows immediately from \cite[Lemma 3.10]{maiza2026lax}.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lem-4: body_md -->
\cite[Lemma 3.11]{maiza2026lax} \label{lem 3.9}
Setting $A = (A_0,A_1,A_2,A_3) = 0,$ the operator \begin{align*}
    L_0: C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g})& \longrightarrow C^{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}) \times \mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}\\
    u& \longmapsto  (-\Ddot{u}, (\delta_{v}(\dot{u}))_{v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}})
\end{align*}
is an isomorphism of Banach spaces.
<!-- end lem-4 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Write $L_A = L_0 + K,$ where $L_0$ is the operator of Lemma \ref{lem 3.9} and \[
    K(u) = (-2\operatorname{ad}_{A_0}\dot{u} - \operatorname{ad}_{\dot{A_0}}u - \operatorname{ad}^{2}_{A_0}u - \sum_{i=1}^{3}\operatorname{ad}^{2}_{A_i}u,0). 
    \]
    
The only term carrying a derivative is $\operatorname{ad}_{A_0}\dot{u}$ (which involves $A_0,$ not the Higgs fields). So $K$ still factors as \[
C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}) \longrightarrow C^{1}(I,\mathfrak{g}) \longrightarrow C^{0}(I,\mathfrak{g})\times \mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}, 
\]
where the first map is compact by Arzel\`a--Ascoli and the second map is bounded. Moreover, we observe that $L_{A} = L_{0}(I + T),$ where $T = L_{0}^{-1}K,$ which is a compact operator on $C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}).$ By the Fredholm alternative, either $I + T$ is invertible or there exists $u\neq 0$ such that $(I + T)u = 0.$ In the latter case, $L_{A}u = 0,$ which contradicts the injectivity of $L_{A}$ (Lemma \ref{injectivity}). Hence $(I + T)$ is invertible, and therefore $L_A$ is an isomorphism.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- def-2: body_md -->
\label{de 4.1}
    Let $I_1,I_2,I_3$ act on $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ by left quaternion multiplication on the second factor of \ref{eq}, i.e., by $i,j \text{ and } k$ respectively. In components, \begin{align*}
        I_{1}(X_0,X_1,X_2,X_3) &= (-X_1,X_0,-X_3,X_2)\\
        I_{2}(X_0,X_1,X_2,X_3) &= (-X_2,X_3,X_0,-X_1)\\
        I_{3}(X_0,X_1,X_2,X_3) &= (-X_3,-X_2,X_1,X_0) 
        \end{align*}
<!-- end def-2 -->
<!-- lem-5: body_md -->
\label{tri hlm}
    The operators $I_1,I_2 \text{ and } I_3$ satisfy the quaternion relations $I^{2}_{1} = I^{2}_{2} = I^{2}_{3} = -\text{id}$ and $I_{1}I_{2} = I_3, I_{2}I_{3} = I_1 \text{ and } I_{3}I_{1} = I_2;$ each $I_i$ is a $\langle \cdot ,\cdot \rangle$-isometry, and each $\omega_i$ is a constant (hence closed) weakly non-degenerate $2$-form. Consequently, $(\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma),\langle \cdot,\cdot \rangle, I_1,I_2,I_3)$ is a hyperkähler Banach manifold.
<!-- end lem-5 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The relations follow because $I_a$ is left multiplication by the $a$-th imaginary unit and left multiplication is an algebra homomorphism $\mathbb{H} \longrightarrow \text{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{H}).$ Thus $I_{a}I_{b} = L_{q_{a}}L_{q_{b}} = L_{q_{a}q_{b}}$, where $(q_1,q_2,q_3) = (i,j,k),$ and $ij = k, \quad i^{2} = -1,$ etc. Each $L_{q_a}$ preserves the standard quaternionic form on $\mathbb{H}$ and $\operatorname{Ad}$-invariance handles the $\mathfrak{g}$-factor, so $I_a$ is an isometry. A constant form is closed; weak non-degeneracy is immediate from $\omega_{a}(X,\cdot) = \langle I_{a}X,\cdot \rangle$ and the invertibility of $I_a.$
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lem-6: body_md -->
(Tri-holomorphic isometric action)  $\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma)$ acts on $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ by tri-holomorphic isometries: each $g\in \mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma)$ preserves $\langle \cdot,\cdot \rangle$ and commutes with $I_1, I_2,I_3.$
<!-- end lem-6 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
The term $\dot{g}g^{-1}$ lies only in the $A_0$ part and is independent of $A,$ so it drops out of the differential. Hence \[
    dg_{A}(X) = (\operatorname{Ad}_{g}X_0, \operatorname{Ad}_{g}X_1, \operatorname{Ad}_{g}X_2, \operatorname{Ad}_{g}X_3) = (\operatorname{Ad}_{g} \otimes \mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{H}})X,
    \]
    under \ref{eq}. Now, $\operatorname{Ad}_{g}$ acts on the $\mathfrak{g}$-factor, while $\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{H}}$ and each $I_{a} = L_{q_a}$ act on the $\mathbb{H}$-factor; operators acting on distinct tensor factors commute, so $dg_{A}(X) \circ I_{a} = I_{a} \circ dg_{A}(X).$ Since $\operatorname{Ad}_g$ is orthogonal and acts diagonally on $(X_0,X_1,X_2,X_3),$ $dg_{A}$ is a $\langle \cdot, \cdot \rangle$-isometry.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- thm-2: body_md -->
Let $(M,g,I_1,I_2,I_3)$ be a finite-dimensional hyperkähler manifold with Kähler forms $\omega_1,\omega_2,\omega_3$, and let $K$ be a Lie group acting by preserving the hyperkähler structure and admitting an equivariant moment map $\mu = (\mu_1,\mu_2,\mu_3): M \longrightarrow \mathfrak{k}^{*} \otimes \mathbb{R}^{3}, d\langle \mu_{a},\xi \rangle = i_{\xi_M}\omega_{a}.$ If $K$ acts freely on $\mu^{-1}(0)$ and the action is proper, then $M///K \coloneqq \mu^{-1}(0)/K$ is a hyperkähler manifold of dimension $\dim M - 4\dim K.$
<!-- end thm-2 -->
<!-- thm-3: body_md -->
\label{infinite red} 
Let $(\mathcal{B},\omega)$ be a weak symplectic Banach manifold carrying a symplectic action of a Banach Lie group $K$ with equivariant moment map $\mu: \mathcal{B} \longrightarrow \mathfrak{k}^{*}.$ Suppose that \begin{enumerate}
    \item (Regular value). $0$ is a regular value of $\mu,$ so that $ \mathcal{A} = \mu^{-1}(0)$ is a Banach submanifold with $T_{A}\mathcal{A} = \text{Ker} (d_{A}\mu).$
    \item (Free and proper). The $K$-action on $\mathcal{A}$ is free and proper. 
    \item (Slices). The $K$-action on $\mathcal{A}$ admits local slices.
    \item (Orthogonality). For each $A\in \mathcal{A},$ the orbit tangent space satisfies $T_{A}(K\cdot A) = (T_{A}\mathcal{A})^{\perp_{\omega}},$ where $W^{\perp_{\omega}} = \{Z: \omega(Z,Y) = 0, \quad \forall Y \in W\}.$ \label{cd 4}
\end{enumerate}
    Then, the quotient $\mathcal{M} = \mathcal{A}/K$ is a smooth manifold and carries a unique symplectic form $\omega_{\text{red}}$ with $\pi^{*}\omega_{\text{red}} = i^{*}\omega,$ where $i: \mathcal{A} \hookrightarrow \mathcal{B}$ and $\pi:\mathcal{A} \longrightarrow \mathcal{M}.$
<!-- end thm-3 -->
<!-- rem-1: body_md -->
The definition of the moment map already gives the inclusion $T_{A}(K\cdot A) \subset (T_{A}\mathcal{A})^{\perp_{\omega}}:$ indeed, $\omega(D_{A}u,Y) = d_{A}\langle \mu,u \rangle(Y) = 0$ for $Y \in \ker d_{A}\mu.$ The content of condition \ref{cd 4} is the reverse inclusion, which fails in general in the weak setting and must be proved. This is precisely the content of \cite[P. 123]{MarsdenWeinstein} and of Lemma \ref{lemma 4.10} below.
<!-- end rem-1 -->
<!-- prop-5: body_md -->
\label{q slice} Suppose, in the situation of Theorem \ref{infinite red} applied simultaneously to $\omega_1,\omega_2$ and $\omega_3,$ that for each $A\in \mu^{-1}(0),$ the metric-orthogonal model of the reduced tangent space \[
T_{[A]}\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \cong \text{Ker} (D^{*}_{A}) \coloneqq \text{im}(D_{A})^{\perp} \cap T_{A}\mu^{-1}(0)
\]
is invariant under each $I_{a}.$ Then, the reduced forms $\omega_{a,\text{red}},$ the reduced metric, and the induced operators $I_{a,\text{red}}$ define a hyperkähler structure on $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma).$
<!-- end prop-5 -->
<!-- prop-6: body_md -->
\label{moment iden} For every $a \in \{1,2,3\},$ every $u\in \operatorname{Lie}(\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma))$ and every $A\in \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma),$ \[
d_{A}\langle \mu_{a},u \rangle(X) = \omega_{a}(D_{A}u,X) \quad \text{ for all } X \in T_{A}\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma).
\]
Hence, $\mu$ is a hyperkähler moment map for the $\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma)$-action.
<!-- end prop-6 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
We give the full proof in the case $a = 1;$ the cases $a=2,3$ are identical after relabeling. Differentiating the first component of $\langle \mu_1,u\rangle$ yields 
\begin{equation}
    d_{A}\bigr( \int_{I} \langle u,\dot{A}_{1} + [A_0,A_1] + [A_2,A_3] \rangle \bigl)(X) = \int_{I} \langle u, \dot{X}_{1} + [X_0,A_1] + [A_0,X_1] + [X_2,A_3] + [A_2,X_3] \rangle dt.
\end{equation}
Using integration by parts over each edge $e$, the term $\int_{I} \langle u,\dot{X}_1 \rangle dt$ becomes \begin{equation}
    \int_{I} \langle u, \dot{X}_1 \rangle dt = \sum_{e\in E} \bigr( \langle u^{e}(b_e),X_{1}(b_e) \rangle - \langle u^{e}(a_e),X_1(a_e) \rangle \bigl) - \int_{I} \langle \dot{u},X_1 \rangle dt . 
\end{equation}

Using the matching conditions $u^{e}(a_e) = u(s(e)), u^{e}(b_e) = u(t(e))$ and $u(v) = 0$ on $\partial \Gamma,$ the boundary sum equals \[
\sum_{v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}} \bigg\langle u(v), \sum_{e\in t^{-1}(v)} X^{e}_{1}(b_e) - \sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)} X^{e}_{1}(a_e) \bigg\rangle = -d_{A}(\text{  vertex part of } \langle \mu_1,u \rangle)(X).
\]

Thus, \begin{align*}
d_{A}\langle \mu_1,u \rangle (X) = \int_{I} \bigr( -\langle \dot{u},X_1 \rangle + \langle u,[X_0,A_1] + [A_0,X_1] + [X_2,A_3] + [A_2,X_3] \rangle dt \bigl). 
\end{align*}

By reorganizing each term using $\operatorname{Ad}$-invariance in the form $\langle u,[Y,Z] \rangle = \langle [u,Y],Z \rangle$ and the skew-symmetry $\langle u,[Y,Z] \rangle  = -\langle u,[Z,Y] \rangle :$

\begin{align*}
\langle u,[X_0,A_1] \rangle &= -\langle [u,A_1],X_0 \rangle, \quad \langle u,[A_0,X_1] \rangle = \langle [u,A_0],X_1 \rangle,\\
\langle u,[X_2,A_3] \rangle &= -\langle [u,A_3],X_2 \rangle, \quad \langle u,[A_2,X_3] \rangle = \langle [u,A_2],X_3 \rangle, 
\end{align*}

one gets \[
d_{A}\langle \mu_1,u \rangle(X) = \int_{I} \bigr( -\langle [u,A_1],X_0 \rangle + \langle [u,A_0] - \dot{u},X_1 \rangle - \langle [u,A_3],X_2 \rangle + \langle [u,A_2],X_3 \rangle \bigl).
\]

On the other hand, by Definition \ref{de 4.1} and \ref{DA}, \[
I_{1}D_{A}u = (-[u,A_1],[u,A_0] - \dot{u},-[u,A_3],[u,A_2]),
\]
so that \[
\omega_{1}(D_{A}u,X) = \langle I_{1}D_{A}u,X \rangle = \int_{I} \bigr( -\langle [u,A_1],X_0 \rangle + \langle [u,A_0] - \dot{u},X_1 \rangle - \langle [u,A_3],X_2  \rangle + \langle [u,A_2],X_3 \rangle \bigl) dt.
\]

The two expressions agree, proving $d_{A}\langle \mu_1,u \rangle = \omega_1(D_{A}u,\cdot).$ Equivariance of $\mu$ follows from the $\operatorname{Ad}$-invariance of the inner product together with Lemma \ref{tri hlm}.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lem-7: body_md -->
\label{holo map}
For each cyclic $(a,b,c) \in \{(1,2,3),(2,3,1),(3,1,2)\}$, set \[
\mu_{+}^{a} \coloneqq \mu_{b} + i\mu_{c}: \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \longrightarrow C^{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}) \times \mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}_{\mathbb{C}}.
\]     
Then, $\mu_{+}^{a}$ is holomorphic with respect to the complex structure $I_{a}$ on $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma).$ Equivalently, for any $A\in \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma), X\in T_{A}\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ and $u\in C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}),$ \begin{equation} \label{eq 7}
    d_{A}\langle \mu^{a}_{+},u \rangle (I_{a}X) = id_{A}\langle \mu^{a}_{+}, u \rangle(X).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-7 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
By the moment map identity of Proposition \ref{moment iden}, $d_{A}\langle \mu_{d},u \rangle (Y) = \omega_{d}(D_{A}u,Y) = \langle I_{d}D_{A}u,Y\rangle$ for every $d\in \{1,2,3\}.$ Hence, \[
    d_{A}\langle \mu^{a}_{+},u \rangle(X) = \langle I_{b}D_{A}u,X \rangle + i\langle I_{c}D_{A}u,X\rangle.
    \]
Replacing $X$ by $I_{a}X$ and using that each $I_{d}$ is a $\langle ,\rangle$-isometry (Lemma \ref{tri hlm}), we have $I_{a}^{*} = I_{a}^{-1} = -I_{a},$ so that $\langle I_{d}D_{A}u,I_{a}X \rangle = - \langle I_{a}I_{d}D_{A}u,X \rangle.$ The quaternion relations $I_{a}I_{b} = I_c$ and $I_{a}I_{c} = -I_{b}$ give \[
\langle I_{b}D_{A}u,I_{a}X \rangle = -\langle I_{c}D_{A}u,X \rangle, \quad \langle I_{c}D_{A}u,I_{a}X \rangle = \langle I_{b}D_{A}u,X\rangle.
\]

Therefore, \[
d_{A}\langle \mu^{a}_{+},u \rangle(I_{a}X) = -\langle I_{c}D_{A}u,X \rangle + i\langle I_{b}D_{A}u,X \rangle = i(\langle I_{b}D_{A}u,X \rangle + i\langle I_{c}D_{A}u,X \rangle),
\]
which is $\ref{eq 7}.$
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- cor-1: body_md -->
\label{kah mani}For each $a\in \{1,2,3\},$ the level set \[
N^{a} \coloneqq (\mu_{+}^{a})^{-1}(0) = \mu_{b}^{-1}(0) \cap \mu_{c}^{-1}(0)
\]
is a complex Kähler Banach submanifold of $(\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma),I_{a}),$ and the restrictions $$(N^{a},\langle \cdot,\cdot \rangle_{|N^{a}},I_{a}|_{N^{a}},\omega_{a}|_{N^{a}})$$ form a (weak) Kähler Banach manifold. The action of $\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma)$ preserves $N^{a}$ and is by Kähler isometries, with moment map $\mu_{a}|_{N^{a}}.$ Moreover, $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) = (\mu_{a}|_{N^{a}})^{-1}(0).$
<!-- end cor-1 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{holo map}, $\mu_+^{a}$ is
$I_a$-holomorphic. By Proposition \ref{regular value}, $0$ is a regular value of the combined map $\mu=(\mu_1,\mu_2,\mu_3)$; since the projection onto the $(\mu_b,\mu_c)$-components preserves surjectivity of the differential, $0$ is a regular value of $\mu_+^{a}$, so $N^a$ is a Banach submanifold of $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ with
\(T_AN^{a} = \ker d_A\mu_b \cap \ker d_A\mu_c\). The
holomorphicity \eqref{eq 7} forces $T_AN^{a}$ to be
$I_a$-stable, so $N^a$ inherits an integrable complex structure
(the complex structure $I_a$ on the ambient space $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ is constant, hence trivially integrable, and a complex submanifold of a complex Banach manifold is complex).
 
The restriction $\omega_a|_{N^{a}}$ is the closed, weakly
non-degenerate form $\langle I_a\cdot,\cdot\rangle$ on the
complex submanifold; compatibility with the restricted metric is
inherited from $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$. Since $\mathcal{G}_0(\Gamma)$ acts on $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ by tri-holomorphic isometries (Lemma \ref{tri hlm}) and preserves
$\mu_b^{-1}(0)\cap\mu_c^{-1}(0)$ by equivariance of $\mu_b,\mu_c$, it
preserves $N^a$ and acts by Kähler isometries. The restriction of
the hyperkähler moment map identity (Proposition \ref{moment iden}) to $T_AN^{a}$
gives \(d_A\langle\mu_a,u\rangle(X) = \omega_a(D_Au,X)\) for
$X\in T_AN^{a}$, so $\mu_a|_{N^{a}}$ is a moment map for the
$\mathcal{G}_0(\Gamma)$-action on the Kähler manifold $N^{a}$. The final
identity is the tautology
$\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) = \mu^{-1}(0) = \mu_a^{-1}(0)\cap N^{a}$.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lem-8: body_md -->
\label{lemma 4.10} For every $A\in \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ and every $a\in \{1,2,3\},$ \begin{equation} \label{eq 8}
T_{A}(\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma)\cdot A) = (T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma))^{\perp_{\omega_{a}}} \cap T_{A}N^a,
\end{equation}
where the symplectic orthogonal is computed with respect to $\omega_{a}|_{N^a}.$
<!-- end lem-8 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
\noindent\textbf{Step 1: Topological splitting of} $T_{A}N^a.$ We extend Proposition \ref{prop 3.6} from $T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma).$ The operator \[
L_{A} \coloneqq D_{A}^{*}D_{A}: C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}) \longrightarrow C^{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}) \times \mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}
    \]
is an isomorphism of Banach spaces (Proposition \ref{prop 3.6}); write $\Pi \coloneqq D_{A}L_{A}^{-1}D^{*}_{A}$ for the associated bounded projector on $T_{A}\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma),$ so that $\text{im}(\Pi) = \text{im}(D_{A})$ and $\text{im}(1 - \Pi) = \text{Ker}(D^{*}_{A}).$ Since the tangent space to the orbit $T_{A}(\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma)\cdot A) = \text{im}(D_A)$ is contained in $T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \subset T_{A}N^a,$ both $\Pi$ and $1 - \Pi$ preserve $T_{A}N^a.$ Therefore,
\begin{equation} \label{eq 9}
    T_{A}N^a = \text{im} D_{A} \oplus (\text{Ker} D^{*}_{A} \cap T_{A}N^a),
\end{equation}
    as a closed topological sum.\\

\noindent\textbf{Step 2: Easy inclusion} ($\supseteq$ in \ref{eq 8}). For $u\in C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g})$ and $Y\in T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma),$ the moment map identity (Proposition \ref{moment iden}) and $Y \in \ker d_{A}\mu_{a}$ give \[
\omega_{a}(D_{A}u,Y) = d_{A}\langle \mu_{a},u \rangle(Y) = 0,
\]
so $\text{im}(D_{A}) \subseteq (T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma))^{\perp_{\omega_a}} \cap T_{A}N^a.$\\


\noindent\textbf{Step 3: Hard inclusion} ($\subseteq$ in \ref{eq 8}). Let $X \in T_{A}N^a$ with $\omega_{a}(X,Y) = 0$ for all $Y \in T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma).$ By \ref{eq 9}, we write \[
X = D_{A}u + Z, \quad u = L_{A}^{-1}(D^{*}_{A}X) \in C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}), \quad Z \in \text{Ker} D^{*}_{A} \cap T_{A}N^a.
\]
We claim that $Z = 0.$ By Step 2, $\omega_{a}(D_{A}u,Y) = 0$ for all $Y\in T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma),$ hence \begin{equation} \label{eq 10}
    \omega_{a}(Z,Y) = 0, \text{ for every } Y \in T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma).
\end{equation}
Now, we verify three properties of $Z:$ 
\begin{itemize}
    \item \noindent (i) $D^{*}_{A}(I_{a}Z) = 0.$ For every $v\in C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}), D_{A}v \in \text{im} D_{A} \subset T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma),$ so \ref{eq 10} gives \[
    0 = \omega_{a}(Z,D_{A}v) = \langle I_{a}Z,D_{A}v \rangle = \langle D^{*}_{A}(I_{a}Z),v \rangle.
    \] 
    The pairing on the right-hand side, between $C^{0}(I,\mathfrak{g})\times \mathfrak{g}^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}$ and $C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}),$ is continuously non-degenerate, so $D^{*}_{A}(I_{a}Z) = 0.$
    \item \noindent (ii) $I_{a}Z \in T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma):$ we verify the three conditions $d_{A}\mu_{d}(I_{a}Z) = 0$ for $d = a,b,c.$ For every $v\in C^{2}_{0}(I,\mathfrak{g}),$ using that the $I_d$ are isometries ($I_{d}^{*} = -I_{d}$) and the quaternion relations $I_{a}I_{b} = I_{c}$ and $I_{a}I_{c} = -I_{b}:$  
    
\begin{align*}
  d_A\langle\mu_a,v\rangle(I_aZ)
  &= \langle I_aD_Av,I_aZ\rangle = \langle D_Av,Z\rangle
   = \langle v,D_A^*Z\rangle = 0,
  \quad\text{since }Z\in\ker D_A^*; \\
  d_A\langle\mu_b,v\rangle(I_aZ)
  &= \langle I_bD_Av,I_aZ\rangle = -\langle I_aI_bD_Av,Z\rangle
   = -\langle I_cD_Av,Z\rangle \\
  &= -d_A\langle\mu_c,v\rangle(Z) = 0,
  \quad\text{since }Z\in T_AN^{a}\subset\ker d_A\mu_c; \\
  d_A\langle\mu_c,v\rangle(I_aZ)
  &= \langle I_cD_Av,I_aZ\rangle = -\langle I_aI_cD_Av,Z\rangle
   = \langle I_bD_Av,Z\rangle \\
  &= d_A\langle\mu_b,v\rangle(Z) = 0,
  \quad\text{since }Z\in T_AN^{a}\subset\ker d_A\mu_b.
\end{align*}

\item \noindent (iii) $Z\in T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma):$ it remains to verify that
$d_A\mu_a(Z)=0$. For every $u\in C^2_0(I,\mathfrak g)$, using $I_a^*=-I_a$
and~(i),
\[
  d_A\langle\mu_a,u\rangle(Z)
  = \omega_a(D_Au,Z) = \langle I_aD_Au,Z\rangle
  = -\langle D_Au,I_aZ\rangle = -\langle u,D_A^*(I_aZ)\rangle = 0.
\]
\end{itemize}
We now conclude. By (ii), $I_{a}Z \in T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma).$ Applying \eqref{eq 10} to $Y = I_{a}Z$ gives \[
0 = \omega_{a}(Z,I_{a}Z) = \langle I_{a}Z,I_{a}Z \rangle = \|I_{a}Z\|^{2}_{L_2}.
\]    
Since the $L_{2}$ inner product is positive definite, $I_{a}Z = 0,$ hence $Z = 0.$ Thus, $X = D_{A}u \in \text{im}D_{A} = T_{A}(\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma)\cdot A),$ which completes the proof of \ref{eq 8}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- rem-2: body_md -->
\label{rmq 4.12}
    Step 3(ii) of the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma 4.10} establishes that $I_{a}$ maps $T_{A}N^a \cap \text{Ker}(D^{*}_{A})$ into $T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \cap \text{Ker}(D^{*}_{A});$ the analogous computation with $Z \in T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \cap \text{Ker}(D^{*}_{A})$ in place of $Z\in T_{A}N^{a}$ shows that \[
    I_{a}(T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \cap \text{Ker}(D^{*}_{A})) \subset T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \cap \text{Ker}(D^{*}_{A}) \quad (a = 1,2,3),
    \]
    which is precisely the hypothesis of Proposition \ref{q slice} (quaternionic slice criterion). This hypothesis is therefore a consequence of Lemmas \ref{holo map}--\ref{lemma 4.10} and the topological splitting of Proposition \ref{prop 3.6}.
<!-- end rem-2 -->
<!-- thm-4: body_md -->
\label{complex q}
    Let $\Gamma$ be an oriented connected quiver with boundary and $G$ a compact connected Lie group. For any solution $(\alpha,\beta)$ to the complex Nahm equations, there exists $g \in \mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ such that $g\cdot (\alpha,\beta )$ is a solution to the real Nahm equation. Moreover, $g$ is unique up to left multiplication by $\mathcal{G}^{0}(\Gamma).$ In other terms, the inclusion $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \hookrightarrow \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ descends to a biholomorphism \[
    \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \coloneqq \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)/\mathcal{G}^{0}(\Gamma) \longrightarrow \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)/\mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma) \coloneqq \mu^{-1}_{\mathbb{C}}(0)/\mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma).
    \]
<!-- end thm-4 -->
<!-- def-3: body_md -->
A smooth function $\phi: H\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ is \textit{geodesically convex} if for any geodesic $\gamma: \mathbb{R} \longrightarrow H,$ the composition $\phi \circ \gamma$ is convex in the usual sense: $(\phi \circ \gamma)^{''}(t) \geq 0, \text{ for all } t.$ It is \textit{strictly geodesically convex} if $(\phi \circ \gamma)^{''}(t) > 0$ for all $t.$
<!-- end def-3 -->
<!-- prop-7: body_md -->
\label{potential}
    For each $x\in \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}},$ the function $V_{x}: H\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ defined by \[
    V_{x}(h) = \|\operatorname{Ad}_{h}(x)\|^{2},
    \]
    where $h\in G_{\mathbb{C}}$ is any representative of the coset and the norm is computed using the $\operatorname{Ad}_{G}$-invariant inner product on $\mathfrak{g}$ extended to $\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}},$ is well-defined, non-negative, smooth, and geodesically convex.
<!-- end prop-7 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Well-definedness follows from the $\operatorname{Ad}_{G}$-invariance of the norm. Non-negativity and smoothness are immediate. For geodesic convexity, let $\gamma(t) = G\exp(itw)g$ be a geodesic in $H$ for $w\in \mathfrak{g}$ and $g\in G_{\mathbb{C}}.$ Then, \[
\dfrac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}} V_{x}(\gamma(t)) = 2 \|[w,\operatorname{Ad}_{\exp(itw)g}x]\|^{2} \geq 0.
\]  
This establishes geodesic convexity.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lem-9: body_md -->
\label{lem mini}
For every $p,q \in H$ and $x\in \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}},$ there exists $h^{*}\in C^{2}([0,1],H)$ with $h^{*}(0) = p,\ h^{*}(1) = q$ minimizing the action
\[
S(h) \coloneqq \int_{0}^{1} \|\dot{h}\|_{H}^{2} + V_{x}(h(t)) \, dt.
\]
The minimizer satisfies the Euler--Lagrange equation
\begin{equation} \label{euler eq}
2\nabla_{\dot{h}^{*}}\dot{h}^{*} = \mathrm{grad} V_{x}(h^{*}).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-9 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
The fact that the critical points of $S$ satisfy the Euler--Lagrange equation $\eqref{euler eq}$ is straightforward. To show that minimizers exist, we proceed as follows.
     Consider a minimizing sequence $(h_n)_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ for $S,$ i.e. $S(h_n) \longrightarrow \inf S.$ Since $V_x \geq 0,$
\[
\int_{0}^{1} \|\dot{h}_{n}\|^{2}\,dt \leq \int_{0}^{1} \|\dot{h}_{n}\|^{2} + V_x(h_n(t))\,dt = S(h_n),
\]
so there is a constant $C>0$ with $\int_{0}^{1} \|\dot{h}_{n}\|^{2}\,dt < C.$ By Cauchy--Schwarz,
\[
d_H(h_n(t),p) \leq \int_{0}^{t} \|\dot{h}_{n}(s)\|\,ds \leq \Big(\int_{0}^{1} \|\dot{h}_{n}\|^{2}\,dt\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}} \leq C^{\frac{1}{2}},
\]
so $h_n([0,1]) \subset \overline{B_H}(p,C^{\frac{1}{2}}).$ The curves are moreover equicontinuous:
\[
d_H(h_n(t),h_n(s)) \leq \int_{s}^{t} \|\dot{h}_{n}(r)\|\,dr \leq C^{\frac{1}{2}}|t-s|^{\frac{1}{2}}.
\]
By Arzel\`a--Ascoli, there is a uniformly convergent subsequence $h_{n_k} \longrightarrow h_*$ with $h_*(0)=p$ and $h_*(1)=q.$ Then $\int_{0}^1 V_{x}(h_{n_k}) \longrightarrow \int_{0}^1 V_{x}(h_*)$ and, by lower semicontinuity of the Dirichlet energy for curves into a Riemannian manifold, $\int_{0}^{1} \|\dot{h}_{*}\|^{2} \leq \liminf \int_{0}^{1} \|\dot{h}_{n_k}\|^{2}.$ Hence
\[
S(h_*) = \int_{0}^1 \|\dot{h}_{*}(t)\|^{2} + V_{x}(h_{*}(t))\,dt \leq \liminf \int_{0}^1 \|\dot{h}_{n_k}\|^2 + V_{x}(h_{n_k})\,dt = \liminf S(h_{n_k}) \leq \inf S,
\]
so $h_*$ is a minimizer and a weak solution of \eqref{euler eq}, which is an ODE on $H.$ A standard bootstrap argument shows that $h_*$ is smooth.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- prop-8: body_md -->
\label{first var}
Consider $\phi(s)=S(h_s)$. Then
\[
\phi^{'}(s) =\delta(S)_{h_s}(\dot{h}_s)= 2\langle \dot{h}_{s}(1),\dot{\gamma}_{2}(s) \rangle - 2\langle \dot{h}_{s}(0),\dot{\gamma}_{1}(s) \rangle,
\]
where $\delta(S)_{h_s}(\dot{h}_s)$ is the variation of $S$ at $h_s$ in the direction of $\dot{h}_s$.
<!-- end prop-8 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Differentiating,
\[
\dot{\phi}(s) = \int_{0}^1 2\langle \nabla_{s}\dot{h}_{s},\dot{h}_{s} \rangle + dV_{x}(J) \, dt.
\]
Since the Levi-Civita connection is torsion-free and $[\partial_{s},\partial_t] = 0,$
\begin{equation} \label{equ torsion free}
\nabla_{s}\dot{h}_{s} = \nabla_{s}\partial_{t}\Phi = \nabla_{t}\partial_{s}\Phi = \nabla_t J.
\end{equation}
Hence $\phi^{'}(s) = \int_{0}^{1} 2\langle \nabla_{t}J,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle + dV_{x}(J)\,dt.$ Integrating the first term by parts,
\[
\int_{0}^{1} 2\langle \nabla_{t}J,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle \, dt = 2[\langle J,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle]^{1}_{0} - \int_{0}^{1} 2\langle J,\nabla_{t}\dot{h}_s \rangle \, dt.
\]
Using the Euler--Lagrange equation $2\nabla_{t}\dot{h}_{s} = \mathrm{grad} V_{x}(h_s)$ and $dV_{x}(J) = \langle \mathrm{grad} V_x, J\rangle,$
\[
\phi^{'}(s) = 2[\langle J,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle]^{1}_{0} -\int_{0}^{1} \langle \mathrm{grad} V_x, J \rangle + \int_{0}^{1} \langle \mathrm{grad} V_x, J\rangle \, dt = 2[\langle J, \dot{h}_s \rangle]^{1}_{0}.
\]
Substituting the boundary conditions \eqref{boundary conditions} gives the result.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- prop-9: body_md -->
\label{s va}
We have
\[
\phi^{''}(s) = \int_{0}^{1} 2\|\nabla_{t}J \|^{2} + 2\langle R(J,\dot{h}_{s})J,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle + \mathrm{Hess}(V_x)(J,J)\,dt,
\]
where $R$ is the Riemann curvature tensor of $H.$
<!-- end prop-9 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
We differentiate $\phi^{'}(s) = \int_{0}^{1} 2\langle \nabla_{t}J,\dot{h}_s \rangle$ with respect to $s,$ treating each term separately. Set $K_{s} \coloneqq \nabla_{s}J.$

\noindent\textbf{Term 1.} For the kinetic term,
\[
\dfrac{d}{ds} \langle \nabla_{t}J,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle = \langle \nabla_{s}\nabla_{t}J,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle + \langle \nabla_{t}J,\nabla_{s}\dot{h}_s \rangle.
\]
By definition of the Riemann curvature tensor, $\nabla_{s}\nabla_{t}J - \nabla_{t}\nabla_{s}J = R(\partial_{s}\Phi,\partial_{t}\Phi)J = R(J,\dot{h}_{s})J.$ Combined with \eqref{equ torsion free},
\[
\dfrac{d}{ds} \langle \nabla_{t}J,\dot{h}_s \rangle = \langle \nabla_{t}K_s,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle + \langle R(J,\dot{h}_{s})J,\dot{h}_s \rangle + \|\nabla_{t}J\|^{2}.
\]

\noindent\textbf{Term 2.} For the potential term,
\[
\dfrac{d}{ds} dV_{x}(J) = \langle \nabla_{s} \mathrm{grad} V_{x},J \rangle + \langle \mathrm{grad} V_{x},K_{s} \rangle = \mathrm{Hess}(V_x)(J,J) + dV_{x}(K_s).
\]
Integrating and adding gives
\[
\phi^{''}(s) = \int_{0}^{1} \Big( 2\| \nabla_{t}J \|^{2} + 2\langle R(J,\dot{h}_{s})J,\dot{h}_s \rangle + \mathrm{Hess}(V_x)(J,J) \Big)dt + \underbrace{\int_{0}^{1} 2\langle \nabla_{t}K_s,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle + dV_{x}(K_s)\,dt}_{(*)}.
\]
We claim that $(*) = 0.$ Integrating the first part of $(*)$ by parts,
\[
\int_{0}^{1} 2\langle \nabla_{t}K_{s},\dot{h}_{s}\rangle \, dt = 2[\langle K_{s},\dot{h}_{s} \rangle]^{1}_{0} - \int_{0}^{1} 2\langle K_{s},\nabla_{t}\dot{h}_{s} \rangle = 2[\langle K_{s},\dot{h}_{s} \rangle]^{1}_{0} - \int_{0}^{1} dV_{x}(K_s)\,dt,
\]
where we used the Euler--Lagrange equation in the second step. Hence $(*) = 2[\langle K_{s},\dot{h}_{s} \rangle]^{1}_{0}.$ Finally, at $t = 0,$ $\Phi(s,0) = \gamma_{1}(s),$ so $K_{s}(0) = \nabla_{s}\dot{\gamma}_{1}(s) = 0$ since $\gamma_{1}$ is a geodesic; at $t = 1,$ similarly, $K_{s}(1) = 0.$ Therefore $(*) = 0,$ which completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- cor-2: body_md -->
\label{fixed endpoints}
For two smooth geodesics $\gamma_1,\gamma_2$ with $\gamma_i(0)=p$ and $\gamma_i(1)=q$ for $i=1,2$, we have $\phi^{'}(s)=0$ and
\[
\phi^{''}(s) =\operatorname{Hess}(S)(J,J)= \int_{0}^{1} 2\|\nabla_{t}J \|^{2} + 2\langle R(J,\dot{h}_{s})J,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle + \mathrm{Hess}(V_x)(J,J)\,dt.
\]
<!-- end cor-2 -->
<!-- lem-10: body_md -->
\label{convexity thm}
With the notation above,
\begin{equation}\label{kt}
\| \nabla_{t}J \|^{2} \geq 0,
\end{equation}
with equality at $t$ if and only if $J$ is parallel along $h_s$ at that point, and
\begin{equation}\label{ct}
\langle R(J,\dot{h}_{s})J,\dot{h}_s \rangle \geq 0,
\end{equation}
with equality at $t$ if and only if either $J$ and $\dot{h}_{s}$ are linearly dependent or the sectional curvature of the plane they span vanishes. In particular, the Kempf--Ness functional is convex along smooth geodesics.
<!-- end lem-10 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
The sectional curvature of the plane $\sigma = \mathrm{span}(J,\dot{h}_{s})$ is
\[
K_{\sigma} = \dfrac{\langle R(J,\dot{h}_{s})\dot{h}_{s},J\rangle}{\|J\|^{2} \|\dot{h}_{s}\|^{2} - \langle J,\dot{h}_{s}\rangle^{2}}.
\]
By the symmetries of the curvature tensor,
\[
\langle R(J,\dot{h}_{s})J,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle = -K(\sigma)\big(\|J\|^{2}\|\dot{h}_{s}\|^{2} - \langle J,\dot{h}_{s} \rangle^{2}\big).
\]
Since $K(\sigma) \leq 0$ on $H$ and the Cauchy--Schwarz factor is non-negative, \eqref{ct} follows. Inequality \eqref{kt} is immediate. Since both integrands are non-negative, $\phi^{''}(s)\ge 0$ by Corollary \ref{fixed endpoints}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- prop-10: body_md -->
\label{jgc}
For each $x\in \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}},$ the function $E_{x}: H\times H \longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ is jointly geodesically convex: for any geodesic $\Gamma(s) = (\gamma_{1}(s),\gamma_{2}(s))$ in $H\times H,$ the composition $s\longrightarrow E_{x}(\gamma_{1}(s),\gamma_{2}(s))$ is convex.
<!-- end prop-10 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
By Corollary \ref{fixed endpoints}, the three integrands of $\phi^{''}(s)$ are non-negative: the first two by \eqref{kt}--\eqref{ct}, and $\mathrm{Hess}(V_x)(J,J)\ge 0$ by the geodesic convexity of $V_x$ (Proposition \ref{potential}). Hence $\phi^{''}(s)\ge 0$ and $\phi$ is convex.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- rem-3: body_md -->
All the previous results remain true if we replace $[0,1]$ by an arbitrary compact interval $[a,b].$
<!-- end rem-3 -->
<!-- rem-4: body_md -->
Joint convexity cannot be upgraded to \textit{joint strict convexity} at the level of a single edge. If $\phi^{''}(s) = 0,$ then $\nabla_{t}J = 0,$ so $J$ is parallel along $h_{s_0}.$ With the boundary conditions $J(0) = \dot{\gamma}_{1}(s_{0})$ and $J(1) = \dot{\gamma}_{2}(s_0),$ neither endpoint forces $J = 0$: the parallel transport of $\dot{\gamma}_{1}(s_0)$ along $h_{s_0}$ from $t=0$ to $t=1$ may equal $\dot{\gamma}_{2}(s_0),$ making the term vanish even when both geodesics are non-constant. The strict convexity for the quiver problem is recovered globally in the next subsection via a propagation argument from the boundary.
<!-- end rem-4 -->
<!-- lem-11: body_md -->
\label{lem uni}
For every $p,q \in H$ and $x\in \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}},$ the solution $h^{*}\in C^{2}([0,1],H)$ with $h^{*}(0) = p,\ h^{*}(1) = q$ to the Euler--Lagrange equation 
\begin{equation}
2\nabla_{\dot{h}^{*}}\dot{h}^{*} = \mathrm{grad} V_{x}(h^{*}),
\end{equation}
minimizing the action
\[
S(h) \coloneqq \int_{0}^{1} \|\dot{h}\|_{H}^{2} + V_{x}(h(t)) \, dt,
\]
is unique, and the map $(p,q) \longrightarrow h^{*}_{p,q}$ depends smoothly on the endpoints.
<!-- end lem-11 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
\noindent\textbf{Uniqueness.} Suppose $h^{*}_{0},h^{*}_1$ are two minimizers. For $\tau \in [0,1],$ let
\[
h_{\tau}(t) = \exp_{h^{*}_{0}(t)}\!\big(\tau \exp^{-1}_{h^{*}_{0}(t)}(h^{*}_{1}(t))\big)
\]
be the pointwise geodesic interpolation, so that $h_0 = h^{*}_{0},\ h_1 = h^{*}_{1},$ and each $h_\tau$ has endpoints $p,q.$ By convexity of the energy density under geodesic interpolation in non-positive curvature, together with the geodesic convexity of $V_x$ (Proposition \ref{potential}),
\[
S(h_{\tau}) \leq (1-\tau)S(h^{*}_{0}) + \tau S(h^{*}_1) \leq S(h^{*}_{0}).
\]
Since $h^{*}_{0}$ is a minimizer, equality holds throughout and $\tau \mapsto S(h_\tau)$ is constant. Let $X(t) = \partial_\tau h_\tau(t)|_{\tau = 0} = \exp^{-1}_{h^{*}_{0}(t)}(h^{*}_{1}(t)),$ so that $X(0) = X(1) = 0.$ Constancy and Corollary \ref{fixed endpoints} give
\[
0=\tfrac{d^2}{d\tau^2}S(h_\tau)\big|_{\tau=0} = \int_{0}^{1} 2\|\nabla_{t}X\|^{2} + 2\langle R(X,\dot{h}^{*}_0)X,\dot{h}^{*}_0\rangle + \mathrm{Hess}(V_x)(X,X)\,dt.
\]
Each integrand is non-negative by \eqref{kt}--\eqref{ct} and Proposition \ref{potential}, so $\nabla_{t}X \equiv 0$; since $X(0) = 0,$ parallelism forces $X \equiv 0,$ hence $h^{*}_{0} = h^{*}_{1}.$\\

\noindent\textbf{Smooth dependence.} Consider the Banach space $\mathcal{P} = \{ h \in C^{2}([0,1],H)\}$ and the map
\[
\mathcal{F}: \mathcal{P} \longrightarrow H \times H \times \mathcal{E}, \quad h \longmapsto \big(h(0),\,h(1),\,2\nabla_{\dot{h}}\dot{h} - \mathrm{grad} V_{x}(h)\big),
\]
so that $\mathcal{F}(h^{*}_{p,q}) = (p,q,0)$. Define $\mathcal{L}: H \times H \times \mathcal{P} \longrightarrow H \times H \times \mathcal{E}$ by $\mathcal{L}(p,q,h) \coloneqq \mathcal{F}(h) - (p,q,0)$. With this definition, we have $\mathcal{L}(p,q,h^{*}_{p,q}) = 0.$ A tangent vector to $\mathcal{P}$ at $h^{*}_{p,q}$ is a $C^2$ vector field $J$ along $h^{*}_{p,q},$ and the derivative of $2\nabla_{\dot{h}}\dot{h} - \mathrm{grad} V_{x}(h)$ is the Jacobi-type operator
\[
LJ \coloneqq 2\nabla^{2}_{t}J + 2R(J,\dot{h}^{*})\dot{h}^{*} - \nabla_{J}\mathrm{grad} V_{x},
\]
so that
\[
D_{h^{*}_{p,q}}\mathcal{F}(J) = \big(J(0),\,J(1),\,LJ\big).
\]
For $v_{0},v_1 \in T_{p}H \times T_{q}H$ and $f$ a vector field along $h^{*}_{p,q},$ the equation $D_{h^{*}_{p,q}}\mathcal{F}(J) = (v_0,v_1,f)$ is uniquely solvable if and only if the homogeneous Dirichlet problem $LJ = 0,\ J(0) = J(1) = 0$ has only the trivial solution. Pairing $LJ$ with $J$ and integrating by parts on the interval $[0,1],$ we obtain
\[
0= \int_{0}^{1} \langle LJ,J \rangle \, dt =-\operatorname{Hess}(S)(J,J) = \int_{0}^{1} 2\|\nabla_{t}J\|^{2} + 2\langle R(J,\dot{h}^{*})J,\dot{h}^{*}\rangle + \mathrm{Hess}(V_x)(J,J) \, dt.
\]
By the convexity argument above, we obtain $\nabla_{t}J \equiv 0,$ and $J(0) = 0$ forces $J \equiv 0.$ Hence $D_{h^{*}_{p,q}}\mathcal{F}$ is a Banach-space isomorphism, and by the implicit function theorem there is a smooth map $(p',q') \longrightarrow h^{*}_{p',q'}$ near $(p,q)$ solving $\mathcal{F}(h) = (p',q',0).$ By uniqueness of the minimizer, this is $h^{*}_{p',q'},$ which establishes smooth dependence.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- rem-5: body_md -->
\label{weak H1 existence}
The existence of the minimizer in Lemma \ref{lem mini} can equally be obtained by weak compactness. Since $V_x \geq 0,$ the functional $S$ is bounded below by $\int_{0}^{1} \|\dot{h}\|^{2} \geq d_{H}(p,q)^{2},$ and a minimizing sequence satisfies $\int_{0}^{1} \|\dot{h}_{n}\|^{2} \leq C.$ Since $H$ is complete, simply connected, and of non-positive curvature, the Sobolev space $H^{1}([0,1],H)$ with fixed endpoints is weakly compact (see, e.g., Jost \cite{jost2005riemannian}), so $h_n$ converges weakly to some $h^{*}$ in $H^{1}.$ The compact embedding $H^{1}([0,1],H) \hookrightarrow C^{0}([0,1],H)$ upgrades this to uniform convergence, whence $\int_{0}^1 V_{x}(h_n) \longrightarrow \int_{0}^1 V_{x}(h^{*})$ by continuity of $V_x$ and dominated convergence; weak lower semicontinuity of the Dirichlet energy then gives $S(h^{*}) \leq \liminf S(h_n) \leq \inf S.$
<!-- end rem-5 -->
<!-- def-4: body_md -->
The \textbf{quiver Kempf--Ness functional} is \begin{equation}
        F((h_e)_{e\in E}) = \sum_{e\in E} \int_{a_e}^{b_e} (\|\dot{h}_{e}\|^{2}_{H} + V_{x_{e}}(h_{e}(t)))dt,
    \end{equation}

    defined on the space $\mathcal{P}$ of tuples $(h_{e})_{e\in E}$ with $h_{e} \in C^{2}([0,1],H)$ such that: \begin{itemize}
        \item at each boundary vertex $v\in \partial \Gamma,$ the endpoint value is prescribed by the boundary data;
        \item at each interior vertex $v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}$, $h_{e_1}(b_e) = h_{e_{2}}(a_e)$ for all $e_{1},e_{2}$ meeting at $v.$
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end def-4 -->
<!-- def-5: body_md -->
Let $\Gamma = (E,V,s,t)$ be a connected quiver with non-empty boundary and interior vertices $\Gamma_{\text{int}} = V-{\partial \Gamma}.$ Fix $x^{e}\in \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}.$ The \textbf{reduced Kempf--Ness functional} is \[
    \bar{F}: H^{|\Gamma_{\text{int}}|} \longrightarrow \mathbb{R}, \quad \bar{F}(p) \coloneqq \sum_{e\in E} E_{x_{e}}(p_{s(e)},p_{t(e)}),
    \]
    where $p = (p_{v})_{v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}},$ and boundary values are used when $s(e)$ or $t(e)$ lies in $\partial \Gamma$.
<!-- end def-5 -->
<!-- thm-5: body_md -->
\label{kempf ness}
    Let $\Gamma$ be a connected quiver with non-empty boundary, and fix $x^e \in \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}$ for each edge and boundary values for each $v\in \partial \Gamma.$ Then $\bar{F}$ is strictly geodesically convex. In particular, $\bar{F}$ has a unique minimum.
<!-- end thm-5 -->
<!-- prop-11: body_md -->
The Riemannian product $H^{N}$ (for any $N \geq 1$) is a simply connected, complete Riemannian manifold of non-positive sectional curvature. In particular, it is a Cartan--Hadamard manifold.
<!-- end prop-11 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Simple connectedness and completeness follow from the corresponding properties of $H.$ For the curvature, let $(u_1,...,u_N)$ and $(v_1,...,v_N)$ be orthonormal tangent vectors at a point of $H^{N}.$ The sectional curvature of the product is \[
    K((u)_i,(v)_i) = \dfrac{\sum_{i=1}^N K_{i}(u_i,v_i)\| u_{i} \wedge v_{i}\|^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^N \|u_{i}\wedge v_{i}\|^2 + \sum_{i<j} \| u_{i}\wedge v_{j} + u_{j}\wedge v_{i}\|^{2}} \leq 0,
    \]
    since each $K_i \leq 0.$
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- prop-12: body_md -->
\label{gc}
    The reduced Kempf--Ness functional $\bar{F}: H^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}} \longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ is geodesically convex.
<!-- end prop-12 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Geodesics on the Riemannian product $H^{|\Gamma_{\text{int}}|}$ are products of geodesics: if $\gamma(s) = (\gamma_{v}(s))_{v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}},$ each $\gamma_{v}$ is a geodesic in $H.$ For each edge $e\in E,$ the values $(\gamma_{s(e)}(s),\gamma_{t(e)}(s))$ trace a geodesic in $H \times H,$ where boundary vertices contribute constant components (a constant path is a geodesic with zero velocity). By Proposition \ref{jgc}, each summand $s\longrightarrow E_{x_{e}}(\gamma_{s(e)}(s),\gamma_{t(e)}(s))$ is convex. Since a sum of convex functions is convex, \[
    \dfrac{d^2}{ds^{2}} \bar{F}(\gamma(s)) = \sum_{e\in E} \dfrac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}} E_{x^{e}}(\gamma_{s(e)}(s),\gamma_{(t(e))}(s)) \geq 0.
    \]
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- prop-13: body_md -->
\label{sgc}
    The reduced functional $\bar{F}: H^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}} \longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ is strictly geodesically convex: for every non-constant geodesic $\gamma(s)$ in $H^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}, \dfrac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}} \bar{F}(\gamma(s)) > 0.$
<!-- end prop-13 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Let $\gamma(s) = (\gamma_{v}(s))_{v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}}$ be a non-constant geodesic, so that at least one component $\gamma_{v_{*}}$ is non-constant. For each edge $e\in E,$ let $h^{e}_{s}$ be the minimizing path from $\gamma_{s(e)}(s)$ to $\gamma_{t(e)}(s)$ (using the fixed boundary value when $s(e)$ or $t(e)$ lies in $\partial \Gamma$, which counts as a constant geodesic). Let $J^{e} \coloneqq \partial_{s}h^{e}_s$ be the variation field along $h^{e}_{s}.$\\ By Proposition \ref{s va} applied to each edge, \[
    \dfrac{d^2}{ds^{2}}\bar{F}(\gamma(s)) = \sum_{e\in E} \int_{a_e}^{b_e} 2\|\nabla_{t}J^{e}\|^{2} + 2\langle R(J^{e},\dot{h}_{e})J^{e},\dot{h}^{e}_{s} \rangle + \mathrm{Hess}(V_x)(J^{e},J^{e})dt.
    \] 

Each term in the sum is non-negative by Proposition \ref{potential} and Lemma \ref{convexity thm}. Suppose, for contradiction, that the total vanishes at some $s_{0}.$ Then for every edge: \begin{itemize}
    \item [(a)] $\nabla_{t}J^{e} \equiv 0, i.e., J^e$ is parallel along $h^{e}_{s_0};$ 
    \item [(b)] $\langle R(J^e,\dot{h}^{e}_{s_0})J^e,\dot{h}^{e}_{s_0} \rangle \equiv 0$ on $[a_e,b_e];$
    \item [(c)] $\mathrm{Hess}(V_{x_e})(J^e,J^e) \equiv 0$ on $[a_e,b_e].$
\end{itemize}
We use only condition $(a)$ and the boundary conditions to derive a contradiction.\\

\noindent\textbf{Propagation from the boundary.} Since $\Gamma$ is connected with non-empty boundary, choose any boundary vertex $v_0 \in \partial \Gamma$ and the unique edge $e_0$ adjacent to $v_0.$ We treat the case $s(e_0) = v_0$ (the case $t(e_0) = v_0$ is analogous). The boundary value at $s(e_0)$ is fixed, so \[
J^{e_0}(a_e) = \dot{\gamma}_{s(e_0)}(s_0) = 0.
\]
Combined with $(a),$ $J^{e_0}$ is parallel along $h^{e_0}_{s_0}$ with $J^{e_0} = 0,$ hence $J^{e_0}\equiv 0$ on $[a_e,b_e].$ In particular, \[
J^{e_0}(b_e) = \dot{\gamma}_{t(e_0)}(s_0) = 0.
\]

We have shown that $\dot{\gamma}_{t(e_0)}(s_0) = 0.$

\noindent\textbf{Inductive step.} We propagate this conclusion through the quiver. Let $W \subset V$ be the set of vertices $v$ for which we have established $\dot{\gamma}_{v}(s_0) = 0$ (where $\dot{\gamma}_{v}(s_0) = 0$ holds trivially for $v\in \partial \Gamma$). After the previous step, $\partial \Gamma \cup \{t(e_0)\} \subset W.$\\

Suppose $W \neq V.$ Since $\Gamma$ is connected, there exists an edge $e_1\in E$ with one endpoint in $W$ and the other not in $W.$ Without loss of generality, $s(e_1)\in W$ and $t(e_1) \notin W$ (the opposite case is symmetric). Then $J^{e_1}(a_e) = \dot{\gamma}_{s(e_1)}(s_0) = 0.$ By $(a),$ $J^{e_1}$ is parallel with $J^{e_1}(a_e) = 0,$ hence $J^{e_1}\equiv 0.$ In particular, $\dot{\gamma}_{t(e_1)}(s_0) = J^{e_1}(b_e) = 0,$ so $t(e_1)\in W.$ This enlarges $W.$\\

Iterating, we obtain $W = V$ in finitely many steps (since $V$ is finite). Therefore $\dot{\gamma}_{v}(s_0) = 0$ for every $v\in V,$ in particular for $v_{*},$ contradicting the assumption that $\gamma_{v_{*}}$ is non-constant.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- prop-14: body_md -->
\label{proper}
    The reduced functional $\bar{F}$ is proper: $\bar{F}(p) \longrightarrow \infty$ as $p\longrightarrow \infty$ in $H^{|\Gamma_{\text{int}}|}.$
<!-- end prop-14 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
For any $p,q \in H$ and $x\in \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}},$ the lower bound $V_x \geq 0$ gives \[
E_{x}(p,q) \geq \inf \Big\{ \int_{a}^{b} \|\dot{h}\|^2 dt : h(a) = p,  h(b) = q \Big\} = d_{H}(p,q)^2,
\]

the energy of the unique minimizing geodesic from $p$ to $q.$ Suppose $p = (p_v)_{v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}}$ tends to infinity in $H^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}$, i.e., $\|p_{v_{*}}\| \longrightarrow \infty$ for some $v_{*} \in \Gamma_{\text{int}}.$ Since $\Gamma$ is connected with non-empty boundary, there exists a path in $\Gamma$ from $v_{*}$ to a boundary vertex. Along this path, the values $p_v$ go from a divergent value at $v_{*}$ to a fixed value in $\partial \Gamma,$ so at least one edge $e$ along the path satisfies $d_{H}(p_{s(e)},p_{t(e)})^2 \longrightarrow \infty.$ Then \[
\bar{F}(p) \geq E_{x^{e}}(p_{s(e)},p_{t(e)}) \geq d_{H}(p_{s(e)},p_{t(e)})^{2} \longrightarrow \infty.
\]
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- rem-6: body_md -->
\label{rem:complex_properness}
In contrast to the unitary gauge group $\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma)$, whose action on $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ is proper by virtue of the compactness of the target Lie group $G$ combined with an Arzel\`a--Ascoli bootstrap on the gauge differential equation (Proposition \ref{proper gauge}), the complexified gauge group $\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^{0}(\Gamma)$ is modeled on the non-compact complex reductive Lie group $G_{\mathbb{C}}$. Consequently, properness of the $\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^{0}(\Gamma)$-action on the complex locus $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma) = \mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}(0)$ does not follow from elementary compactness arguments and is not automatic \emph{a priori}.

This is precisely where the Kempf--Ness framework on the Cartan--Hadamard manifold $H^{|\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}|}$ (with $H = G_{\mathbb{C}}/G$) becomes essential. For any solution $(\alpha, \beta) \in \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$, the reduced functional $\overline{F} : H^{|\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}|} \to \mathbb{R}$ is proper (Proposition \ref{proper}) and strictly geodesically convex via the boundary propagation principle (Proposition \ref{sgc} and Theorem \ref{kempf ness}). Strict convexity ensures the existence and uniqueness of a global harmonic minimizer $p^*$, establishing that each $\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^{0}(\Gamma)$-orbit contains a unique $\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma)$-orbit of solutions lying in the unitary locus $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$.

Furthermore, the properness of $\overline{F}$ guarantees that any gauge sequence $(g_n) \subset \mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^{0}(\Gamma)$ along which configurations converge remains confined to a compact set modulo $\mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma)$. The action of $\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^{0}(\Gamma)$ on the stable locus $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ is therefore proper, its orbits are closed, and the resulting complex quotient $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma) = \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)/\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^{0}(\Gamma)$ inherits a canonical smooth complex manifold structure for which the projection map is a holomorphic submersion.
<!-- end rem-6 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
By Proposition \ref{gc}, $\bar{F}$ is geodesically convex. By Proposition \ref{sgc}, it is strictly geodesically convex. By Proposition \ref{proper}, it is proper. A proper, strictly geodesically convex function on a Cartan--Hadamard manifold attains its infimum at a unique point: existence follows from properness, and uniqueness follows from strict convexity (two minima on a geodesic would contradict strict convexity along the geodesic).
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Given $(\alpha,\beta)$ satisfying the complex Nahm equation on each edge and Kirchhoff matching for $\beta,$ we gauge $\alpha^e$ to zero on each edge, producing constants $x^e \in \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}$ and holonomies $a^e \in G_{\mathbb{C}}.$ The quiver Kempf--Ness functional $\bar{F}$ on $H$ admits a unique minimum $p^{*} = (p^{*}_{v})_{v\in \Gamma_{\text{int}}}$ by Theorem \ref{kempf ness}. For each edge $e,$ the minimizing path $h^{*}_{e}: [0,1]\longrightarrow H$ from $p^{*}_{s(e)}$ to $p^{*}_{t(e)}$ (with boundary values as appropriate) lifts to a path $g^e \in C^{2}([0,1],G_{\mathbb{C}}).$ The Euler--Lagrange equation for $h^{*}_{e}$ translates precisely into the real Nahm equation for $g^{e} \cdot (0,x^e).$ The matching at interior vertices ensures that $g = (g^e)_{e\in E} \in \mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma),$ and the boundary conditions give $g\in \mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ (up to the initial gauging by $g_0$). For uniqueness, suppose that $g,g^{'} \in \mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ produce solutions to the full Nahm equations. Then, $F(g) = F(g^{'})$ implies that the corresponding junction points in $H^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}$ are both critical points of $\bar{F}.$ By uniqueness of the minimizer, they agree. On each edge, the minimizing path is unique, so $g$ and $g^{'}$ project to the same paths in $H^{E},$ i.e., $g^{'} = kg$ for some $k\in \mathcal{G}_{0}(\Gamma).$ \\

Now for the biholomorphism: the construction yields a bijection \[
\Phi: \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \longrightarrow \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)\mkern-4mu\mathbin{/\mkern-5mu/}_{\mkern-4mu{}}\mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma), \quad [A] \longmapsto [(A_0 + iA_1,A_2 + iA_3)]
\]
induced by the inclusion $i:\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \hookrightarrow \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma).$ We verify that $\Phi$ is a biholomorphism. 

The inclusion $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)\cong\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}(\Gamma)$ 
is smooth, and the projection $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ 
is a smooth submersion: the linear differential arguments and slice decompositions of Section \ref{section 3} adapt directly to $\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}$, while properness and 
freeness of the non-compact gauge action $\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^{0}(\Gamma)$ on the stable locus $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ are guaranteed by Remark \ref{rem:complex_properness}. Composing, $\Phi$ is smooth. Moreover, the inverse $\Phi^{-1}(\alpha,\beta)$ is given by $h^{*}g\cdot(\alpha,\beta)\in \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma),$ where $g\cdot(\alpha,\beta)_{|e} = (0,x^{e}), g_{e}(a_e) = 1$ and $h^{*}$ is the unique minimizer of $\bar{F}.$ The map $(\alpha,\beta) \longrightarrow (x^{e})_{e\in E}$ is smooth by the ODE; the boundary data of $\bar{F}$ depend smoothly on $(\alpha,\beta)$; and the minimizer $p^{*} = p^{*}(\alpha,\beta)$ depends smoothly on these data. To see this, consider the map \[
\Psi: \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma) \times H^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}} \longrightarrow T^{*}H^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}, \quad \Psi((c,d),p) \longmapsto d_{(c,d)}\bar{F}(p).
\] 
This map is smooth: $(x^{e})$ depends smoothly on $(c,d)$ by smooth dependence of ODE solutions on parameters, and $E_{x}(p,q)$ is jointly smooth in $(x,p,q)$ (Proposition \ref{jgc}, applied with $x$ as an additional parameter). At the critical point $p^{*} = p^{*}(\alpha,\beta),$ we have $d_{(\alpha,\beta)}\bar{F}(p^{*}) = 0,$ and the partial derivative $\partial_{p^*} \Psi$ equals $\mathrm{Hess}\bar{F}(p^*),$ which is positive definite as a bilinear form by strict geodesic convexity (Proposition \ref{sgc}), and hence invertible because $H^{\Gamma_{\text{int}}}$ is finite-dimensional. The implicit function theorem yields a smooth map $(c,d) \longrightarrow p^{*}(c,d)$ locally near $(\alpha,\beta).$ Finally, the per-edge minimizers depend smoothly on $p^*$ by the last point of Lemma \ref{lem uni}. Composing, $\Phi^{-1}$ is smooth.\\

By Corollary \ref{kah mani}, $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma) = \mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}(0) = N^1$ is an $I_1$-complex Kähler Banach submanifold of $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma),$ and $\mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ acts holomorphically. Hence $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)\coloneqq \mu_{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}(0)/\mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ inherits a complex structure $\tilde{I}_1,$ characterised by the requirement that the projection $N^{1} \longrightarrow \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ be holomorphic.\\ On the other hand, the descended complex structure $I_{1,\text{red}}$ on $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$ defined by the quaternionic slice criterion (Proposition \ref{q slice}) and Lemma \ref{lemma 4.10} has the horizontal model \[
T_{[A]}\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \cong T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \cap \text{Ker} D_{A}^{*},
\]
on which $I_1$ acts (Remark \ref{rmq 4.12}). Since the inclusion $T_{A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \hookrightarrow T_{A}N^{1} = \text{Ker} d_{A}\mu_{\mathbb{C}}$ is $I_1$-linear (both are $I_1$-stable subspaces of $T_{A}\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma)$), the differential \[
d_{[A]}\Phi: T_{[A]}\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) \longrightarrow T_{[(\alpha,\beta)]}\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)
\]
intertwines $I_{1,\text{red}}$ with $\tilde{I}_{1}.$ As $d_{[A]}\Phi$ is a bounded, $I_1$-linear isomorphism, $\Phi$ is a biholomorphism.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- prop-15: body_md -->
\label{prop:length-invariance-complex}
For any two length assignments $l,l_1 \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^E$, there exists a canonical biholomorphism and holomorphic symplectomorphism
\[
\Phi_{l,l_1}^* : \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma_{l}) \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ } \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma_{l_1})
\]
preserving the boundary evaluation moment maps. Consequently, $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ is independent of the edge lengths.
<!-- end prop-15 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Without loss of generality, let $l_1 = \mathbf{1} = (1)_{e\in E}$ be the unit length vector with standard intervals $J_e = [0, 1]$. For each $e \in E$, consider the affine orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
\[
\phi_e : J_e \longrightarrow I_e, \qquad s \longmapsto a_e + s l_e.
\]
We identify the complex connection $\alpha^e$ with a Lie-algebra-valued $1$-form $\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}^e = \alpha^e(t)\,dt$ on $I_e$, and the Higgs field $\beta^e$ with a Lie-algebra-valued $0$-form (a section of $I_e \times \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}$). We define the pullback map on fields $\Phi^* : \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma_{l}) \to \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{1}})$ edge-wise by
\[
\tilde{\alpha}^e(s)\,ds := \phi_e^*(\alpha^e(t)\,dt) = l_e\,\alpha^e(a_e + s l_e)\,ds, \qquad \tilde{\beta}^e(s) := \phi_e^*(\beta^e)(s) = \beta^e(a_e + s l_e).
\]
We verify the invariance of each component of the reduction:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Complex Nahm Equation:} The equation $\dot{\beta}^e + [\alpha^e, \beta^e] = 0$ is equivalent to the vanishing of the covariant exterior derivative $d_{\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}^e}\beta^e := d\beta^e + [\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}^e, \beta^e] = 0$ on $I_e$. Since the exterior derivative and the Lie bracket commute with smooth pullbacks,
    \[
    \phi_e^*(d\beta^e + [\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}^e, \beta^e]) = d\tilde{\beta}^e + [\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbb{C}}^e, \tilde{\beta}^e] = \left(\frac{d\tilde{\beta}^e}{ds}(s) + [\tilde{\alpha}^e(s), \tilde{\beta}^e(s)]\right)ds = 0.
    \]
    Thus, $\Phi^*$ preserves the complex Nahm equation edge-wise.

    \item \textbf{Kirchhoff Matching:} The complex Kirchhoff condition at any $v \in \Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}$ depends only on endpoint values:
    \[
    \sum_{e \in s^{-1}(v)} \beta^e(a_e) - \sum_{e \in t^{-1}(v)} \beta^e(b_e) = \sum_{e \in s^{-1}(v)} \tilde{\beta}^e(0) - \sum_{e \in t^{-1}(v)} \tilde{\beta}^e(1) = 0.
    \]
    Hence, $\Phi^*(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma_{l})) = \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{1}}).$

    \item \textbf{Gauge Equivariance:} The complex gauge group $\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^0(\Gamma_{l})$ pulls back via $\tilde{g}^e(s) = g^e(\phi_e(s))$ to $\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^0(\Gamma_{\mathbf{1}})$. Since $\phi_e(0) = a_e$ and $\phi_e(1) = b_e$, vertex matchings and boundary triviality conditions are preserved. The gauge action transforms as
    \[
    \phi_e^*(g \cdot \alpha_{\mathbb{C}}^e) = \phi_e^*(-\dot{g}g^{-1}dt + g\alpha^e g^{-1}dt) = -\left(\frac{d\tilde{g}}{ds}\tilde{g}^{-1}\right)ds + \tilde{g}\tilde{\alpha}^e\tilde{g}^{-1}ds = \tilde{g} \cdot \tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbb{C}}^e.
    \]
    Thus, $\Phi^*$ is $\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^0$-equivariant.

    \item \textbf{Holomorphic Symplectic Form:} The holomorphic symplectic form $\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^l = \omega_2 + i\omega_3$ on $\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma_{l})$ is given on variations $(\delta\alpha, \delta\beta), (\delta'\alpha, \delta'\beta)$ by
    \[
    \Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{l}(\delta A, \delta' A) = \sum_{e\in E} \int_{a_e}^{b_e} \Big( \langle \delta\alpha^e(t), \delta'\beta^e(t) \rangle - \langle \delta'\alpha^e(t), \delta\beta^e(t) \rangle \Big)\,dt.
    \]
    Changing variables $t = a_e + s l_e$ with $dt = l_e\,ds$, and substituting $\delta\tilde{\alpha}^e(s) = l_e\,\delta\alpha^e(\phi_e(s))$ and $\delta\tilde{\beta}^e(s) = \delta\beta^e(\phi_e(s))$, the Jacobian factor $l_e$ cancels identically:
    \[
    \int_{a_e}^{b_e} \langle \delta\alpha^e(t), \delta'\beta^e(t) \rangle\,dt = \int_0^1 \left\langle \frac{1}{l_e}\delta\tilde{\alpha}^e(s), \delta'\tilde{\beta}^e(s) \right\rangle l_e\,ds = \int_0^1 \langle \delta\tilde{\alpha}^e(s), \delta'\tilde{\beta}^e(s) \rangle\,ds.
    \]
    Thus, $\Phi^* \Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{l} = \Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathbf{1}}$.
\end{enumerate}
Since $\Phi^*$ is an equivariant, symplectic isomorphism of the ambient Banach manifolds preserving the moment map zero loci, it descends to a canonical holomorphic symplectomorphism $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma_{l}) \cong \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{1}}).$
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- rem-7: body_md -->
The result becomes equally transparent once $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ is identified with a finite-dimensional complex symplectic quotient (Theorem \ref{thm:finite-dim-reduction} below). On any edge $e$, fixing the gauge $\alpha^e \equiv 0$ implies that $\beta^e(t)$ is constant. The data on $e$ modulo based gauge equivalence are parameterized by the holonomy $g_e := g^{e}_{\alpha}(b_e)$ of Theorem \ref{thm:finite-dim-reduction} and $\xi_e = \beta^e(a_e) \in \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}$, which identifies the unconstrained edge data with $T^* G_{\mathbb{C}}$. Since $\beta^{e}(b_e) = \operatorname{Ad}_{g_e}\xi_e$, the complex Kirchhoff conditions become
\[
\mu_v((g_e, \xi_e)_{e\in E}) = \sum_{e \in s^{-1}(v)} \xi_e - \sum_{e \in t^{-1}(v)} \mathrm{Ad}_{g_e}(\xi_e) = 0 \quad (\forall v \in \Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}).
\]
Because this algebraic condition and the canonical symplectic form on $(T^* G_{\mathbb{C}})^E$ do not depend on $l_e$, the reduction $(T^* G_{\mathbb{C}})^E /\!\!/\!\!/_{\mathbb{C}} G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}} \cong \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ is manifestly independent of the edge lengths.
<!-- end rem-7 -->
<!-- thm-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:finite-dim-reduction}
Let $\Gamma = (E, V, s, t)$ be a connected quiver with non-empty boundary. The map
\[
\varphi : \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma) \longrightarrow (T^*G_{\mathbb{C}})^E /\!\!/\!\!/_{\mathbb{C}} G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}}, \qquad [(\alpha, \beta)] \longmapsto [(g_{\alpha}^e(b_e), \beta^e(a_e))_{e \in E}],
\]
is an isomorphism of complex Hamiltonian $G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial\Gamma}$-spaces, where $g_{\alpha}^e \in C^2(I_e, G_{\mathbb{C}})$ is the unique solution to the parallel transport equation $\dot{g}_{\alpha}^e + \alpha^e g_{\alpha}^e = 0$ with $g_{\alpha}^e(a_e) = \mathbf{1}$.
<!-- end thm-6 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
We follow the construction in \cite[Section 5]{maiza2026lax}, adapted to the complex-holomorphic setting.\\



\textbf{Step 1: Well-definedness and Bijectivity.}
For any solution $(\alpha, \beta) \in \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$, the transformation $g_{\alpha}^{-1} \cdot (\alpha, \beta)$ gauges the connection to zero: $\alpha' = 0$. The complex Nahm equation then reduces to $\dot{\beta}' = 0$, so $\beta'^e(t) \equiv x_e := \beta^e(a_e) \in \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}$ is constant along each edge. Transforming back gives $\beta^e(b_e) = \mathrm{Ad}_{g_{\alpha}^e(b_e)} x_e$. The complex Kirchhoff condition at $v \in \Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}$ becomes
\[
\sum_{e \in s^{-1}(v)} x_e - \sum_{e \in t^{-1}(v)} \mathrm{Ad}_{g_{\alpha}^e(b_e)} x_e = 0,
\]
which is precisely the zero locus of the holomorphic moment map $\nu : (T^*G_{\mathbb{C}})^E \to (\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}^*)^{\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}}$ for the diagonal action of $G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}}$. If $(\alpha, \beta)$ is transformed by $h \in \mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^0(\Gamma)$, the holonomies transform by $h(t(e)) g_{\alpha}^e(b_e) h(s(e))^{-1}$ and $\beta^e(a_e)$ by $\mathrm{Ad}_{h(s(e))}$, which corresponds to the $G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}}$-gauge orbit. Thus, $\varphi$ is well-defined.\\


To construct the smooth inverse, let $(a, x) \in \nu^{-1}(0)$. For each edge $e$, choose a path $\gamma_a^e \in C^2(I_e, G_{\mathbb{C}})$ with $\gamma_a^e(a_e) = \mathbf{1}$ and $\gamma_a^e(b_e) = a_e$. Define
\[
\psi : (T^*G_{\mathbb{C}})^E /\!\!/\!\!/_{\mathbb{C}} G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\Gamma_{\mathrm{int}}} \longrightarrow \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma), \qquad [(a, x)] \longmapsto [(\gamma_a \cdot 0, \gamma_a \cdot x)].
\]
Any other choice $\tilde{\gamma}_a^e$ satisfies $\tilde{\gamma}_a^e(\gamma_a^e)^{-1} \in \mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{C}}^0(\Gamma)$, so the class in $\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma)$ is independent of the choice of path. The moment condition $\nu(a, x) = 0$ guarantees that $\psi(a, x)$ satisfies the Kirchhoff matching at interior vertices. Smooth local sections of $\gamma \mapsto \gamma(b_e)$ guarantee that $\psi$ is smooth, which establishes that $\varphi$ is a biholomorphism.\\



\textbf{Step 2: Holomorphic Symplectic Identification.}
Using the standard coordinates on $T^*G_{\mathbb{C}}$, choose $\gamma_{a e^{s u}}(t) = e^{t y} e^{t s u}$ for $a = e^y$. The differential of $\psi$ is
\[
d\psi_{(a, x)}(u, v) = \left( \mathrm{Ad}_{\gamma_a} u, \, \mathrm{Ad}_{\gamma_a}(s [u, x] + v) \right).
\]
Evaluating the ambient holomorphic symplectic form $\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}$ on two tangent vectors yields
\begin{align*}
(\psi^* \Omega_{\mathbb{C}})_{(a, x)}((u_1, v_1), (u_2, v_2)) &= \int_I \Big( \langle u_1, s [u_2, x] + v_2 \rangle_{\mathbb{C}} - \langle u_2, s [u_1, x] + v_1 \rangle_{\mathbb{C}} \Big) ds \\
&= \langle u_1, v_2 \rangle_{\mathbb{C}} - \langle u_2, v_1 \rangle_{\mathbb{C}} + \langle x, [u_1, u_2] \rangle_{\mathbb{C}},
\end{align*}
which matches the canonical holomorphic symplectic form on $(T^*G_{\mathbb{C}})^E$. Finally, the unreduced boundary values give $\beta(v) = \mathrm{Ad}_{a_e} x_e$ for $v \in \partial\Gamma^+$ and $\beta(v) = x_e$ for $v \in \partial\Gamma^-$, intertwining the residual $G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\partial\Gamma}$ moment maps.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- prop-16: body_md -->
\label{thm:gluing-law}
There exists a canonical biholomorphism and holomorphic symplectomorphism
\[
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\Gamma_1 *_D \Gamma_2) \cong \left( \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma_1) \times \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Gamma_2) \right) /\!\!/\!\!/_{\mathbb{C}}\, G_{\mathbb{C}}^D.
\]
<!-- end prop-16 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
This follows immediately from Theorem \ref{thm:finite-dim-reduction} and reduction by stages.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- prop-17: body_md -->
\label{prop:metric-length-dependence}
Let $\Gamma$ be a connected quiver with non-empty boundary, and assume $\dim G > 0$ and $|E| > |\Gamma_{\text{int}}|$, so that $\dim \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma) > 0$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{(Homothety under uniform rescaling.)} For every $\lambda > 0$ there is a $G^{\partial\Gamma}$-equivariant diffeomorphism $r_\lambda: \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{l}}) \to \mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma_{\lambda\mathbf{l}})$ such that
    \[
    r_\lambda^{*} g_{\mathbb{H},\lambda\mathbf{l}} = \lambda^{-1} g_{\mathbb{H},\mathbf{l}}, \qquad r_\lambda^{*}\omega_{a,\lambda\mathbf{l}} = \lambda^{-1}\omega_{a,\mathbf{l}}, \qquad (r_{\lambda})_{*} I_a = I_a \quad (a=1,2,3),
    \]
    and $\Psi_{\lambda\mathbf{l}} \circ r_\lambda = \delta_{\lambda^{-1}} \circ \Psi_{\mathbf{l}}$. Consequently, $\delta_{\lambda^{-1}}^{*} g^{X}_{\lambda\mathbf{l}} = \lambda^{-1} g^{X}_{\mathbf{l}}$.
    \item \textbf{(Non-triviality.)} Let $x_0 \in X(\Gamma)$ be the class of $(\mathbf{1}_e, 0)_{e\in E}$. For every $\mathbf{l}$, $\Psi_{\mathbf{l}}([0]) = x_0$, and for every $\zeta \in K(\Gamma)$ the class $\hat{\zeta} \in T_{x_0}X(\Gamma)$ of the tangent vector $(0,\zeta_e)_{e\in E}$ satisfies
    \[
    g^{X}_{\mathbf{l}}(\hat{\zeta},\hat{\zeta}) = \sum_{e\in E} l_e\,\|\zeta_e\|^{2}.
    \]
    Consequently, $g^{X}_{\mathbf{l}} \neq g^{X}_{\mathbf{l}'}$ whenever $\sum_{e\in E}(l_e - l'_e)\|\zeta_e\|^2 \neq 0$ for some $\zeta\in K(\Gamma)$; in particular, $g^{X}_{\lambda\mathbf{l}} \neq g^{X}_{\mathbf{l}}$ for every $\lambda \neq 1$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end prop-17 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
\textbf{(1)} For $\lambda > 0$, the edge $I_e = [a_e,b_e]$ of $\Gamma_{\mathbf{l}}$ corresponds to the edge $\lambda I_e = [\lambda a_e,\lambda b_e]$ of $\Gamma_{\lambda\mathbf{l}}$. Define
\[
R_\lambda : \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{l}}) \longrightarrow \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma_{\lambda\mathbf{l}}), \qquad (R_\lambda A)^{e}_{\mu}(t) \coloneqq \lambda^{-1} A^{e}_{\mu}(t/\lambda), \quad \mu = 0,1,2,3,\ t\in \lambda I_e,
\]
and, on gauge groups, $(R_\lambda g)^{e}(t) \coloneqq g^{e}(t/\lambda)$. We check the required properties.
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{Nahm--Kirchhoff space.} For $i\in\{1,2,3\}$,
    \[
    \tfrac{d}{dt}(R_\lambda A)_{i} + [(R_\lambda A)_0,(R_\lambda A)_i] + [(R_\lambda A)_{i+1},(R_\lambda A)_{i+2}] = \lambda^{-2}\bigl(\dot{A}_i + [A_0,A_i] + [A_{i+1},A_{i+2}]\bigr)(t/\lambda),
    \]
    so $R_\lambda$ preserves the Nahm equations. The Kirchhoff conditions are linear and homogeneous in the endpoint values, which $R_\lambda$ multiplies by $\lambda^{-1}$; hence $R_\lambda(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{l}})) = \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma_{\lambda\mathbf{l}})$.
    \item \emph{Gauge equivariance.} $R_\lambda g$ has the same vertex values as $g$, so $R_\lambda$ restricts to isomorphisms $\mathcal{G}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{l}}) \cong \mathcal{G}(\Gamma_{\lambda\mathbf{l}})$ and $\mathcal{G}_0(\Gamma_{\mathbf{l}}) \cong \mathcal{G}_0(\Gamma_{\lambda\mathbf{l}})$. Moreover,
    \[
    (R_\lambda g)\cdot(R_\lambda A)_0 = \lambda^{-1}\bigl(gA_0g^{-1}\bigr)(t/\lambda) - \lambda^{-1}\dot{g}(t/\lambda)g(t/\lambda)^{-1} = \bigl(R_\lambda(g\cdot A)\bigr)_0,
    \]
    and the Higgs components are clearly equivariant. Hence $R_\lambda(g\cdot A) = (R_\lambda g)\cdot (R_\lambda A)$.
    \item \emph{Hyperkähler structure.} By the change of variables $t = \lambda s$,
    \[
    \langle R_\lambda X, R_\lambda Y\rangle = \sum_{e\in E}\int_{\lambda I_e}\sum_{\mu=0}^{3} \lambda^{-2}\langle X_\mu(t/\lambda),Y_\mu(t/\lambda)\rangle\,dt = \lambda^{-1}\langle X,Y\rangle.
    \]
    Since $R_\lambda$ acts on all four components by the same operation, it commutes with $I_1,I_2,I_3$, and hence $R_\lambda^{*}\omega_a = \lambda^{-1}\omega_a$.
\end{itemize}
Consequently, $R_\lambda$ maps $\mathcal{G}_0$-orbits onto $\mathcal{G}_0$-orbits, and since it multiplies the $L^2$ metric by a constant, it maps the metric orthogonal complement $T_A\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{l}})\cap \text{Ker} D^{*}_{A}$ of the orbit onto $T_{R_\lambda A}\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma_{\lambda\mathbf{l}})\cap \text{Ker} D^{*}_{R_\lambda A}$. It therefore descends to a diffeomorphism $r_\lambda$ (with inverse induced by $R_{\lambda^{-1}}$) satisfying the three displayed identities. Equivariance with respect to $G^{\partial\Gamma}$ follows from the fact that $R_\lambda$ preserves boundary values of gauge transformations.

For the finite-dimensional model, write $\alpha = A_0 + iA_1$ and $\beta = A_2 + iA_3$, so that $R_\lambda\alpha = \lambda^{-1}\alpha(\cdot/\lambda)$ and $R_\lambda\beta = \lambda^{-1}\beta(\cdot/\lambda)$. The path $t\mapsto g^{e}_{\alpha}(t/\lambda)$ solves $\dot{g} + \lambda^{-1}\alpha^e(t/\lambda)g = 0$ with value $\mathbf{1}$ at $\lambda a_e$, so the holonomies are unchanged, while $\beta^{e}(\lambda a_e)$ is replaced by $\lambda^{-1}\beta^e(a_e)$. Since $\Phi$ is induced by the inclusion $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}\hookrightarrow \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{C}}$, this gives $\Psi_{\lambda\mathbf{l}}\circ r_\lambda = \delta_{\lambda^{-1}}\circ\Psi_{\mathbf{l}}$. Hence $\Psi_{\lambda\mathbf{l}}^{-1} = r_\lambda\circ \Psi_{\mathbf{l}}^{-1}\circ\delta_\lambda$, and
\[
g^{X}_{\lambda\mathbf{l}} = \delta_\lambda^{*}(\Psi_{\mathbf{l}}^{-1})^{*} r_\lambda^{*} g_{\mathbb{H},\lambda\mathbf{l}} = \lambda^{-1}\delta_\lambda^{*} g^{X}_{\mathbf{l}}.
\]
Applying $\delta_{\lambda^{-1}}^{*}$ to both sides gives $\delta_{\lambda^{-1}}^{*} g^{X}_{\lambda\mathbf{l}} = \lambda^{-1} g^{X}_{\mathbf{l}}$.

\textbf{(2)} The zero configuration $A = 0$ lies in $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{l}})$; its holonomies are trivial and its Higgs fields vanish, so $\Psi_{\mathbf{l}}([0]) = x_0$. Given $\zeta\in K(\Gamma)$, define $Y\in \mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{l}})$ by
\[
Y^{e}_0 = Y^{e}_1 = 0, \qquad Y^{e}_2 + iY^{e}_3 \equiv \zeta_e \quad \text{(constant on } I_e).
\]
At $A = 0$ the linearized Nahm equations reduce to $\dot{Y}_i = 0$, and the real and imaginary parts of $\zeta$ satisfy the Kirchhoff law, so $Y\in T_0\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{H}}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{l}})$. Moreover $D^{*}_0Y = (\dot{Y}_0, (\sum_{s^{-1}(v)}Y^{e}_0(a_e) - \sum_{t^{-1}(v)}Y^{e}_0(b_e))_v) = 0$, so $Y$ is horizontal. By definition of the reduced metric,
\[
g_{\mathbb{H},\mathbf{l}}\bigl(d\pi(Y),d\pi(Y)\bigr) = \|Y\|^2_{L^2} = \sum_{e\in E}\int_{I_e}\bigl(\|Y^{e}_2\|^2 + \|Y^{e}_3\|^2\bigr)dt = \sum_{e\in E} l_e\|\zeta_e\|^2,
\]
where $\|\zeta_e\|^2 = \|Y^{e}_2\|^2 + \|Y^e_3\|^2$ is the Hermitian norm on $\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}$. The variation $Y$ leaves $\alpha$, hence the holonomies, unchanged and varies $\beta^{e}(a_e)$ by $\zeta_e$; thus $d\Psi_{\mathbf{l}}(d\pi(Y)) = \hat{\zeta}$, a tangent vector at $x_0$ which does not depend on $\mathbf{l}$. This proves the formula for $g^{X}_{\mathbf{l}}(\hat{\zeta},\hat{\zeta})$, and the first consequence follows by subtraction. For $\mathbf{l}' = \lambda\mathbf{l}$ with $\lambda\neq 1$, choose any $\zeta\in K(\Gamma)\setminus\{0\}$, which exists since $\dim_{\mathbb{R}}K(\Gamma) = 2(|E|-|\Gamma_{\text{int}}|)\dim G > 0$; then $\sum_e (l_e - \lambda l_e)\|\zeta_e\|^2 = (1-\lambda)\sum_e l_e\|\zeta_e\|^2 \neq 0$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- rem-8: body_md -->
\label{rem:homothety}
Part (1) shows that the inhomogeneous transformation laws obtained when only $A_0$ is treated as a $1$-form and $A_1,A_2,A_3$ as $0$-forms are not, in themselves, an obstruction: treating all four components as $1$-forms, Nahm's equations are invariant under rescaling, and a uniform rescaling of the edge lengths acts on the hyperkähler structure by a homothety, composed with the fibrewise dilation $\delta_{\lambda^{-1}}$. For a single edge, this recovers the fact that the Kronheimer metrics on $T^*G_{\mathbb{C}}$ obtained from intervals of different lengths are homothetic. Part (2) shows that, beyond this homothety, the transported metrics $g^{X}_{\mathbf{l}}$ are genuinely different; Theorem \ref{thm:hklr-metric-gluing} below shows that subdividing an edge while preserving its total length does not change the metric.
<!-- end rem-8 -->
<!-- rem-9: body_md -->
\label{rem:rescaled}
For an edge of length $l$ carrying $(\alpha,\beta) = (0,x)$, we write $E_x(p,q;l)$ for the infimum of $\int_0^l \bigl(\|\dot{h}\|^2_H + V_x(h)\bigr)dt$ over paths $h$ from $p$ to $q$. Setting $\tilde{h}(s) \coloneqq h(sl)$, $s\in[0,1]$, so that $\tilde{h}' = l\dot{h}$, one gets
\[
\int_0^l \bigl(\|\dot{h}\|^2_H + V_x(h)\bigr)dt = \int_0^1 \Bigl(\frac{1}{l}\|\tilde{h}'(s)\|^2_H + l\,V_x(\tilde{h}(s))\Bigr)ds,
\]
and the Euler--Lagrange equation \eqref{euler eq} becomes $\nabla_s\tilde{h}' = \frac{l^2}{2}\mathrm{grad}_H V_x(\tilde{h})$. By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, $E_x(p,q;l) \geq d_H(p,q)^2/l$.
<!-- end rem-9 -->
<!-- def-6: body_md -->
The partial category $\mathbf{Ham}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{pre}}$ is defined as follows:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Objects:} Connected complex reductive Lie groups, with the trivial group $\{*\} = G^0$ serving as the monoidal unit.
    
    \item \textbf{Morphisms:} A morphism $M : G \to H$ is an isomorphism class of smooth holomorphic symplectic manifolds $(M, \Omega)$ equipped with a holomorphic Hamiltonian action of $G \times H$ and an equivariant holomorphic moment map
    \[
    \mu = (\mu_{\mathrm{in}}, \mu_{\mathrm{out}}) : M \longrightarrow \mathfrak{g}^* \times \mathfrak{h}^*.
    \]
    An isomorphism between morphisms $(M, \Omega, \mu)$ and $(M', \Omega', \mu')$ is an equivariant biholomorphism $f: M \to M'$ such that $f^*\Omega' = \Omega$ and $\mu' \circ f = \mu.$
    
    \item \textbf{Composition:} Two morphisms $M : G \to H$ and $N : H \to K$ are \textit{composable} if the diagonal action of $H$ on $\mu_{\Delta}^{-1}(0) \subset M \times N$ is free and proper, where
    \[
    \mu_{\Delta}(x, y) := \mu_{\mathrm{out}}(x) - \mu_{\mathrm{in}}(y) : M \times N \longrightarrow \mathfrak{h}^*.
    \]
    
    In this case, the composite morphism $N \circ M: G \to K$ is defined by the holomorphic symplectic quotient (Marsden--Weinstein reduction):
    \[
    N \circ M := (M \times N) /\!\!/\!\!/_{\mathbb{C}}\, H = \mu_{\Delta}^{-1}(0) / H_{\mathbb{C}}.
    \]
    The residual $G \times K$-action on $\mu_{\Delta}^{-1}(0)$ descends to a holomorphic Hamiltonian action on $N \circ M.$
    \item \textbf{Identity Morphisms:} For any complexified Lie group $G_{\mathbb{C}}$, the identity morphism  $\mathrm{Id}_{G_{\mathbb{C}}} \in \operatorname{Mor}(G_{\mathbb{C}}, G_{\mathbb{C}})$ is the cotangent bundle $T^* G_{\mathbb{C}} \cong G_{\mathbb{C}} \times \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}$ equipped with its canonical holomorphic symplectic form $\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}$ and the Hamiltonian $G_{\mathbb{C}} \times G_{\mathbb{C}}$-action generated by
    \[
    \mu(g, \xi) = (-\xi, \, \mathrm{Ad}_g^* \xi).
    \]
\end{itemize}
<!-- end def-6 -->
<!-- def-7: body_md -->
Applying the Wehrheim--Woodward construction \cite{WehrheimWoodward2010Functoriality} to the partial category $\mathbf{Ham}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{pre}}$ yields the completed category $\mathbf{Ham}_{\mathbb{C}}:$
\begin{itemize}
    \item The objects of $\mathbf{Ham}_{\mathbb{C}}$ are finite Cartesian products of connected complex reductive Lie groups $\prod_{i=1}^n G_{\mathbb{C}}^{(i)}.$
    \item A morphism from $G$ to $H$ is an equivalence class of formal finite sequences $(M_1, M_2, \dots, M_k)$ of composable basic morphisms, modulo the equivalence relation generated by composing adjacent pairs whenever the intermediate holomorphic reduction is smooth (see \cite{maiza2026multiplicative,CrooksMayrand2024MooreTachikawa}).
    \item The monoidal structure is given by the Cartesian product on objects and morphisms, making $\mathbf{Ham}_{\mathbb{C}}$ a symmetric monoidal category (see also \cite{maiza2026multiplicative,maiza2026lax}).
\end{itemize}
<!-- end def-7 -->
<!-- def-8: body_md -->
Every connected oriented cobordism $\Sigma$ with $m$ incoming and $n$ outgoing boundary components deformation-retracts onto an oriented quiver with boundary $\Gamma = (E, V, s, t)$ such that:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item the boundary vertices satisfy $|\partial\Gamma^-| = m$ and $|\partial\Gamma^+| = n;$
    \item the topological thickening $\Sigma(\Gamma)$, obtained by replacing edges with cylinders $I_e \times [-\epsilon, \epsilon]$ and interior vertices with oriented discs glued according to cyclic orderings of incident half-edges, is diffeomorphic to $\Sigma$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end def-8 -->
<!-- prop-18: body_md -->
\label{thm:cutting-gluing}
